+    def invoke_endpoint_async(self) -> TYPE_RESPONSE:
+        endpoint_name = self.path.split("/")[2]
+        input_location = self.headers.get("X-Amzn-SageMaker-InputLocation")
+        inference_id = self.headers.get("X-Amzn-SageMaker-Inference-Id")
+        location = self.sagemakerruntime_backend.invoke_endpoint_async(
+            endpoint_name, input_location
+        )
+        resp = {"InferenceId": inference_id or str(random.uuid4())}
+        headers = {"X-Amzn-SageMaker-OutputLocation": location}
+        return 200, headers, json.dumps(resp)

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple inventory management system using a class named `Inventory`. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Add Item**: Add a specified quantity of an item to the inventory.
2. **Remove Item**: Remove a specified quantity of an item from the inventory. If the item does not exist or the quantity to remove exceeds the available quantity, raise a `ValueError`.
3. **Get Inventory**: Retrieve a copy of the current inventory as a dictionary.
4. **Check Availability**: Check the quantity of a specified item in the inventory. If the item does not exist, return 0.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The `Inventory` class will be tested with a series of operations. Each operation will be one of the four methods described above.
- For the `add_item` and `remove_item` methods, the input will be a string `item` and an integer `quantity`.
- The `get_inventory` method will return a dictionary representing the current state of the inventory.
- The `check_availability` method will return an integer representing the quantity of the specified item.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
inventory = Inventory()
inventory.add_item("apple", 10)
inventory.add_item("banana", 5)
inventory.remove_item("apple", 3)
print(inventory.get_inventory())  # Output: {'apple': 7, 'banana': 5}
print(inventory.check_availability("apple"))  # Output: 7
print(inventory.check_availability("orange"))  # Output: 0
inventory.remove_item("banana", 5)
print(inventory.get_inventory())  # Output: {'apple': 7}
```

```python
class Inventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self.items = {}

    def add_item(self, item, quantity):
        """
        Adds the specified quantity of the item to the inventory.
        
        :param item: The name of the item to add.
        :param quantity: The quantity of the item to add.
        :raises ValueError: If the quantity to add is negative.
        """
        if quantity < 0:
            raise ValueError("Quantity to add cannot be negative")
        if item in self.items:
            self.items[item] += quantity
        else:
            self.items[item] = quantity

    def remove_item(self, item, quantity):
        """
        Removes the specified quantity of the item from the inventory.
        
        :param item: The name of the item to remove.
        :param quantity: The quantity of the item to remove.
        :raises ValueError: If the quantity to remove is negative or if there is not enough of the item in the inventory.
        """
        if quantity < 0:
            raise ValueError("Quantity to remove cannot be negative")
        if item not in self.items or quantity > self.items[item]:
            raise ValueError(f"Not enough {item} in inventory to remove {quantity}")
        self.items[item] -= quantity
        if self.items[item] == 0:
            del self.items[item]

    def get_inventory(self):
        """
        Returns a copy of the current inventory as a dictionary.
        
        :return: A dictionary representing the current state of the inventory.
        """
        return self.items.copy()

    def check_availability(self, item):
        """
        Checks the quantity of a specified item in the inventory.
        
        :param item: The name of the item to check.
        :return: The quantity of the specified item in the inventory, or 0 if the item does not exist.
        """
        return self.items.get(item, 0)

# Minimal example

```python
# publish.py
import boto3

def mqtt_publish(topic: str, payload: bytes):
    iot_data = boto3.client('iot-data', region_name='eu-central-1')
    iot_data.publish(
        topic=topic,
        payload=payload,
        qos=0,
    )
```

E           UnicodeDecodeError: 'utf-8' codec can't decode byte 0xbf in position<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The `Book` class should encapsulate the properties of a book and provide methods to manage its availability. The class should have the following attributes and methods:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.
  - `available`: A boolean indicating whether the book is available for borrowing.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, title, author, isbn)`: Initializes a new book object with the specified title, author, and ISBN. The `available` attribute should be set to `True`.
  - `borrow(self)`: Marks the book as unavailable. If the book is already unavailable, it should raise a `RuntimeError` with the message "Book is already unavailable".
  - `return_book(self)`: Marks the book as available. If the book is already available, it should raise a `RuntimeError` with the message "Book is already available".

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: 
  ```python
  book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935")
  book.borrow()
  print(book.available)  # Output: False
  book.return_book()
  print(book.available)  # Output: True
  book.borrow()
  book.borrow()  # Raises RuntimeError: Book is already unavailable
  ```
- Output:
  ```
  False
  True
  RuntimeError: Book is already unavailable
  ```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn):
        """
        Initializes a new book object with the specified title, author, and ISBN.
        The `available` attribute is set to `True`.
        
        :param title: A string representing the title of the book.
        :param author: A string representing the author of the book.
        :param isbn: A string representing the ISBN of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.available = True

    def borrow(self):
        """
        Marks the book as unavailable. If the book is already unavailable,
        it raises a RuntimeError with the message "Book is already unavailable".
        """
        if not self.available:
            raise RuntimeError('Book is already unavailable')
        self.available = False

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Marks the book as available. If the book is already available,
        it raises a RuntimeError with the message "Book is already available".
        """
        if self.available:
            raise RuntimeError('Book is already available')
        self.available = True
```<#sep#>3→no. n=-1:2 -4 -3= -5→no. n=3:18 +12 -3=27→x=±√27→no. n=-2:8 -8 -3= -3→no. n=4:32 +16 -3=45→x=±√45→no. So no solutions here. Not helpful.

But in terms of pairs (n,x), that's two solutions. If the problem counts each pair as a solution, then even if a is a square, there might be more than one solution. But if a is non-square, like a=2, b=2, r=3, then there are infinitely many solutions.

Therefore, the problem reduces to whether there exists at least one equation of the given form with a, b, r ≠0 which has exactly one integer solution (n,x). If such an equation exists, then the answer is no. Otherwise, if all such equations with at least one solution must have infinitely many, then yes.

From the examples above, when a is a square, we can find equations with finitely many solutions (like the case with a=1, b=4, r=-5, which has two solutions). However, if the problem counts pairs (n,x) as distinct solutions even if x is ±, then those equations still have more than one solution. But the problem says "has one solution", which is a bit ambiguous. But in mathematics, when referring to Diophantine equations, a solution is typically a tuple of all variables. So (n,x) is a solution, and (n,-x) is another solution if x≠0. Thus, unless x=0, which is not possible here because r≠0 (since x² =an² +bn -r, and if x=0, then an² +bn =r. But in the examples we considered earlier, x is non-zero.

But in any case, the problem states that the equation has one solution. If the equation allows x=0, then maybe we can construct an example where there is exactly one solution. <#sep#>You are given a list of tasks for a software project. Each task is represented as a dictionary with the following keys: `'task_id'`, `'task_name'`, `'status'`, and `'priority'`. The `'status'` key can have one of the following values: `'not started'`, `'in progress'`, `'completed'`. The `'priority'` key can have one of the following values: `'low'`, `'medium'`, `'high'`. Your task is to write a function `sort_tasks(tasks)` that sorts the tasks based on the following criteria:
1. Priority in descending order (`'high'` to `'low'`).
2. Status in ascending order (`'completed'` to `'not started'`).
3. Task name in alphabetical order if two tasks have the same priority and status.

**Input:**
- A list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a task with keys `'task_id'`, `'task_name'`, `'status'`, and `'priority'`.

**Output:**
- A list of dictionaries sorted according to the specified criteria.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    {'task_id': 1, 'task_name': 'Design Website', 'status': 'in progress', 'priority': 'high'},
    {'task_id': 2, 'task_name': 'Write Code', 'status': 'not started', 'priority': 'high'},
    {'task_id': 3, 'task_name': 'Test Application', 'status': 'completed', 'priority': 'medium'},
    {'task_id': 4, 'task_name': 'Deploy', 'status': 'not started', 'priority': 'low'},
    {'task_id': 5, 'task_name': 'Write Tests', 'status': 'completed', 'priority': 'medium'},
    {'task_id': 6, 'task_name': 'Update Design', 'status': 'in progress', 'priority': 'medium'},
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[
    {'task_id': 1, 'task_name': 'Design Website', 'status': 'in progress', 'priority': 'high'},
    {'task_id': 2, 'task_name': 'Write Code', 'status': 'not started', 'priority': 'high'},
    {'task_id': 5, 'task_name': 'Write Tests', 'status': 'completed', 'priority': 'medium'},
    {'task_id': 3, 'task_name': 'Test Application', 'status': 'completed', 'priority': 'medium'},
    {'task_id': 6, 'task_name': 'Update Design', 'status': 'in progress', 'priority': 'medium'},
    {'task_id': 4, 'task_name': 'Deploy', 'status': 'not started', 'priority': 'low'},
]
```

```python
def sort_tasks(tasks):
    """
    Sorts a list of task dictionaries based on priority (high to low),
    status (completed to not started), and task name (alphabetical order).

    :param tasks: List of dictionaries, each representing a task.
    :return: Sorted list of task dictionaries.
    """
    def custom_sort_key(task):
        priority_order = {'high': 1, 'medium': 2, 'low': 3}
        status_order = {'completed': 1, 'in progress': 2, 'not started': 3}
        return (priority_order[task['priority']], status_order[task['status']], task['task_name'])
    
    return sorted(tasks, key=custom_sort_key)

</think>To determine whether the equation \( an^2 + bn - x^2 = r \) has infinitely many integer solutions given that it has one solution, we start by rewriting the equation as:

\[ x^2 = an^2 + bn - r. \]

We need to analyze whether the existence of one integer solution implies the existence of infinitely many integer solutions. To do this, we transform the equation into a form that can be analyzed using Pell-type equations.

First, we complete the square for the quadratic term in \( n \):

\[ an^2 + bn = a \left( n + \frac{b}{2a} \right)^2 - \frac{b^2}{4a}. \]

Thus, the equation becomes:

\[ x^2 = a \left( n + \frac{b}{2a} \right)^2 - \frac{b^2}{4a} - r. \]

Multiplying through by \( 4a \) to clear the denominators, we get:

\[ 4a x^2 = 4a^2 \left( n + \frac{b}{2a} \right)^2 - b^2 - 4ar. \]

Let \( m = 2a \left( n + \frac{b}{2a} \right) \), so \( n = \frac{m - b}{2a} \). Substituting back, we obtain:

\[ 4a x^2 = m^2 - b^2 - 4ar. \]

Rearranging, we get:

\[ m^2 - 4a x^2 = b^2 + 4ar. \]

This is a Pell-type equation of the form \( m^2 - D x^2 = C \), where \( D = 4a \) and \( C = b^2 + 4ar \).

The behavior of this Pell-type equation depends on whether \( D = 4a \) is a perfect square or not:

1. **If \( 4a \) is not a perfect square**: The equation \( m^2 - 4a x^2 = C \) is a standard Pell equation, which has infinitely many solutions if it has one solution.

2. **If \( 4a \) is a perfect square**: The equation \( m^2 - 4a x^2 = C \) reduces to a difference of squares, which typically has finitely many solutions.

To illustrate, consider the case where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 4 \), and \( r = -5 \). The equation becomes:

\[ x^2 = n^2 + 4n + 5. \]

We can check for integer solutions:

- For \( n = -2 \):
  \[ x^2 = (-2)^2 + 4(-2) + 5 = 4 - 8 + 5 = 1 \implies x = \pm 1. \]

- For \( n = 0 \):
  \[ x^2 = 0^2 + 4(0) + 5 = 5 \implies x \text{ is not an integer}. \]

- For \( n = 1 \):
  \[ x^2 = 1^2 + 4(1) + 5 = 10 \implies x \text{ is not an integer}. \]

- For \( n = -3 \):
  \[ x^2 = (-3)^2 + 4(-3) + 5 = 9 - 12 + 5 = 2 \implies x \text{ is not an integer}. \]

Thus, the only integer solutions are \( (n, x) = (-2, 1) \) and \( (n, x) = (-2, -1) \). This shows that the equation can have only finitely many solutions even when it has one solution.

Therefore, the existence of one integer solution does not guarantee infinitely many integer solutions. The answer is:

\[
\boxed{\text{No}}
\]<#sep#>.

    # Now, check every possible window in history with length == m = len(S). For each starting index from 0 up to (history_length - m):

Currently, the Moto SNS mock raises an error with code `DuplicateEndpoint` when attempting to create a second platform endpoint using the same token.

This is not actually the behavior on AWS. On AWS, when attempting to do this, when not setting `CustomUserData`, the operation actually returns the existing endpoint.

AWS will raise an exception when `CustomUserData` is set, but the exception raised is of code `InvalidParameter`. Additionally, the exception thrown will have a reason of a specific format: `Endpoint <ARN> already exists with the same Token, but different attributes.` The docs explicitly recommend parsing this reason to get the existing ARN.

References:
- Documentation of current behavior: https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sns/latest/dg/mobile-platform-endpoint.html#mobile-platform-endpoint-sdk-examples
- Documented error types (notably excluding `DuplicateEndpoint`): https://docs.aws.amazon.com/sns/latest/api/API_CreatePlatformEndpoint.html

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class in Python that supports specific operations through its special methods. The class should be initialized with a title and an author. Implement the following methods:

- `__init__(self, title, author)`: Initializes the book with a title and an author.
- `__str__(self)`: Returns a string representation of the book in the format `"Title: <title>, Author: <author>"`.
- `__repr__(self)`: Returns a string that can be used to recreate the object in the format `"Book(title='<title>', author='<author>')"`.
- `__eq__(self, other)`: Compares two books for equality based on their titles and authors.

**Sample Input:**
```python
book1 = Book("1984", "George Orwell")
book2 = Book("1984", "George Orwell")
book3 = Book("Animal Farm", "George Orwell")
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell
Book(title='1984', author='George Orwell')
True
False
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given title and author.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the book.

        :return: A string in the format "Title: <title>, Author: <author>".
        """
        return f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}"

    def __repr__(self):
        """
        Returns a string that can be used to recreate the book object.

        :return: A string in the format "Book(title='<title>', author='<author>')"..
        """
        return f"Book(title='{self.title}', author='{self.author}')"

    def __eq__(self, other):
        """
        Compares two books for equality based on their titles and authors.

        :param other: Another Book object to compare with.
        :return: True if both books have the same title and author, False otherwise.
        """
        if isinstance(other, Book):
            return self.title == other.title and self.author == other.author
        return False

# Example usage:
book1 = Book("1984", "George Orwell")
book2 = Book("1984", "George Orwell")
book3 = Book("Animal Farm", "George Orwell")

print(str(book1))  # Should output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell
print(repr(book1))  # Should output: Book(title='1984', author='George Orwell')
print(book1 == book2)  # Should output: True
print(book1 == book3)  # Should output: False

<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a program to identify duplicate files within a given directory tree. The program should traverse the directory, compute a hash for each file, and report pairs of files that have identical hashes. The directory to be scanned is provided as a command-line argument. Your program should output the paths of duplicate files in the format specified below.

**Input:**
- The program is executed from the command line with a single argument: the path to the directory to be scanned.

**Output:**
- For each pair of duplicate files, print a line in the format: `'<file_path_1>' and '<file_path_2>' are duplicates.'`
- If no duplicates are found, print: `No duplicates found.`

**Sample Input:**
```
python find_duplicates.py /path/to/directory
```

**Sample Output:**
```
'/path/to/directory/file1.txt' and '/path/to/directory/subdir/file1.txt' are duplicates.
'/path/to/directory/image1.png' and '/path/to/directory/image2.png' are duplicates.
No duplicates found.
```

```python
import os
import hashlib
import sys

def hash_file(filename, hash_algo='sha256'):
    """
    Computes the hash of a file using the specified hash algorithm.
    
    :param filename: Path to the file to be hashed.
    :param hash_algo: Hashing algorithm to use (default is 'sha256').
    :return: Hexadecimal digest of the file's hash.
    """
    h = hashlib.new(hash_algo)
    with open(filename, 'rb') as f:
        for chunk in iter(lambda: f.read(4096), b""):
            h.update(chunk)
    return h.hexdigest()

def find_duplicates(root_dir):
    """
    Scans the directory tree for files and groups them by their hash values.
    
    :param root_dir: Root directory to start the search.
    :return: Dictionary mapping file hashes to sets of file paths with that hash.
    """
    seen_hashes = {}
    for dirpath, _, filenames in os.walk(root_dir):
        for filename in filenames:
            file_path = os.path.join(dirpath, filename)
            file_hash = hash_file(file_path)
            if file_hash in seen_hashes:
                seen_hashes[file_hash].add(file_path)
            else:
                seen_hashes[file_hash] = {file_path}
    return seen_hashes

def report_duplicates(seen_hashes):
    """
    Reports pairs of duplicate files based on their hash values.
    
    :param seen_hashes: Dictionary mapping file hashes to sets of file paths.
    """
    duplicates = [file_set for file_set in seen_hashes.values() if len(file_set) > 1]
    if not duplicates:
        print("No duplicates found.")
        return
    duplications = [(list(file_set)[i], list(file_set)[j]) for file_set in duplicates for i in range(len(file_set) - 1) for j in range(i + 1, len(file_set))]
    for a, b in duplications:
        print(f"'{a}' and '{b}' are duplicates.")

def main():
    """
    Main function to execute the program.
    """
    if len(sys.argv) != 2:
        print("Usage: {} dir".format(sys.argv[0]))
        return

    root_dir = sys.argv[1]
    if not os.path.isdir(root_dir):
        print("Directory '{}' doesn't exist!".format(root_dir))
        return

    print(f"Scanning '{root_dir}' for duplicates...\n")
    seen_hashes = find_duplicates(root_dir)
    report_duplicates(seen_hashes)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```<#sep#>² -0 ≡ -6 mod3 → x² + y² ≡ 0 mod3 (since -6 mod3 is 0). So x² + y² ≡0 mod3. As squares modulo3 are 0 or1, the only way their sum is 0 mod3 is if both squares are 0 mod3. <#sep#>You are given a task to convert a JSON file containing a list of products into a CSV file. Each product in the JSON file has three attributes: `name`, `price`, and `description`. Your program should read the JSON file, validate the data, and write the valid products to a CSV file with the headers `name`, `price`, and `description`. The program should handle cases where the JSON file is empty or contains invalid JSON data.

**Input:**
- A JSON file path (`json_file_path`) containing a list of products.
- A CSV file path (`csv_file_path`) where the output should be written.

**Output:**
- A CSV file with the products' data, or an error message if the JSON file is invalid or does not exist.

**Sample Input:**
- JSON file (`products.json`):
  ```json
  [
      {"name": "Laptop", "price": 1200, "description": "High-performance laptop"},
      {"name": "Smartphone", "price": 800, "description": "Latest model smartphone"}
  ]
  ```

**Sample Output:**
- CSV file (`products.csv`):
  ```
  name,price,description
  Laptop,1200,High-performance laptop
  Smartphone,800,Latest model smartphone
  ```

```python
import json
import csv

def json_to_csv(json_file_path, csv_file_path):
    """
    Converts a JSON file containing a list of products to a CSV file.
    
    Parameters:
    json_file_path (str): The path to the input JSON file.
    csv_file_path (str): The path to the output CSV file.
    """
    try:
        with open(json_file_path, 'r') as json_file:
            products = json.load(json_file)
        
        if not isinstance(products, list):
            print("Invalid JSON data format.")
            return

        with open(csv_file_path, 'w', newline='') as csv_file:
            csv_writer = csv.writer(csv_file)
            csv_writer.writerow(['name', 'price', 'description'])
            
            for product in products:
                if not all(key in product for key in ('name', 'price', 'description')):
                    print(f"Invalid product entry: {product}. Skipping...")
                    continue
                
                csv_writer.writerow([product['name'], product['price'], product['description']])
        print(f"Data has been written to {csv_file_path} successfully.")
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The JSON file at {json_file_path} does not exist.")
    except json.JSONDecodeError:
        print(f"Error: The JSON file at {json_file_path} contains invalid JSON data.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An unexpected error occurred: {e}")

</think>To find all integer solutions \( x, y, z \) such that \( x^2 + y^2 - 3z^2 = -6 \), we start by rearranging the equation:

\[
x^2 + y^2 = 3z^2 - 6
\]

First, we observe that the right-hand side must be non-negative:

\[
3z^2 - 6 \geq 0 \implies z^2 \geq 2 \implies |z| \geq 2
\]

Next, we consider the equation modulo 3:

\[
x^2 + y^2 \equiv 3z^2 - 6 \pmod{3} \implies x^2 + y^2 \equiv 0 \pmod{3}
\]

Since the squares modulo 3 are 0 or 1, the only way \( x^2 + y^2 \equiv 0 \pmod{3} \) is if both \( x \) and \( y \) are multiples of 3. Let \( x = 3a \) and \( y = 3b \) for integers \( a \) and \( b \). Substituting these into the original equation gives:

\[
(3a)^2 + (3b)^2 - 3z^2 = -6 \implies 9a^2 + 9b^2 - 3z^2 = -6 \implies 3a^2 + 3b^2 - z^2 = -2 \implies z^2 = 3a^2 + 3b^2 + 2
\]

We now consider \( z^2 \) modulo 3:

\[
z^2 \equiv 3a^2 + 3b^2 + 2 \pmod{3} \implies z^2 \equiv 2 \pmod{3}
\]

However, squares modulo 3 can only be 0 or 1, so \( z^2 \equiv 2 \pmod{3} \) is impossible. This contradiction implies there are no integer solutions to the equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 3z^2 = -6 \).

Thus, the equation has no integer solutions.

\boxed{\text{No solutions}}

    # Implement this code:

    seq = [1]*N

    target = S

    len_target = len(S)

    if N > len_target: # the only possible way is if S is exactly the first N 1's?

       # Thus, for N greater than the length of target, the only possible way the target is subset is if it's the prefix of those 1's.

Alternatively, let me adjust the code to:

def is_part(S, N):

    seq = [1]*N

    m = len(S)

    # Generate sequence up to m + N elements

    required_length = m + N

    while len(seq) < required_length:

        next_term = sum(seq[-N:]) if len(seq)>=N else sum(seq)

        seq.append(next_term)

    # Now, check all possible windows of length m:

    for start in range(len(seq) - m +1):

        if seq[start:start+m] == S:

            return True

    return False

This way, when we generate up to (required_length) which is the sum of N plus the desired window length, then the window can start anywhere in the sequence up to that.

+from .core import TaggedEC2Resource
+from ..utils import generic_filter, random_dedicated_host_id
+from typing import Any, Dict, List
+
+
+class Host(TaggedEC2Resource):
+    def __init__(
+        self,
+        host_recovery: str,
+        zone: str,
+        instance_type: str,
+        instance_family: str,
+        auto_placement: str,
+        backend: Any,
+    ):
+        self.id = random_dedicated_host_id()
+        self.state = "available"
+        self.host_recovery = host_recovery or "off"
+        self.zone = zone
+        self.instance_type = instance_type
+        self.instance_family = instance_family
+        self.auto_placement = auto_placement or "on"
+        self.ec2_backend = backend
+
+    def release(self) -> None:
+        self.state = "released"
+
+    def get_filter_value(self, key):
+        if key == "availability-zone":
+            return self.zone
+        if key == "state":
+            return self.state
+        if key == "tag-key":
+            return [t["key"] for t in self.get_tags()]
+        if key == "instance-type":
+            return self.instance_type
+        return None
+
+
+class HostsBackend:
+    def __init__(self):
+        self.hosts = {}
+
+    def allocate_hosts(
+        self,
+        quantity: int,
+        host_recovery: str,
+        zone: str,
+        instance_type: str,
+        instance_family: str,
+        auto_placement: str,
+        tags: Dict[str, str],
+    ) -> List[str]:
+        hosts = [
+            Host(
+                host_recovery,
+                zone,
+                instance_type,
+                instance_family,
+                auto_placement,
+                self,
+            )
+            for _ in range(quantity)
+        ]
+        for host in hosts:
+            self.hosts[host.id] = host
+            if tags:
+                host.add_tags(tags)
+        return [host.id for host in hosts]
+
+    def describe_hosts(
+        self, host_ids: List[str], filters: Dict[str, Any]
+    ) -> List[Host]:
+        """
+        Pagination is not yet implemented
+        """
+        results = self.hosts.values()
+        if host_ids:
+            results = [r for r in results if r.id in host_ids]
+        if filters:
+            results = generic_filter(filters, results)
+        return results
+
+    def modify_hosts(
+        self, host_ids, auto_placement, host_recovery, instance_type, instance_family
+    ):
+        for _id in host_ids:
+            host = self.hosts[_id]
+            if auto_placement is not None:
+                host.auto_placement = auto_placement
+            if host_recovery is not None:
+                host.host_recovery = host_recovery
+            if instance_type is not None:
+                host.instance_type = instance_type
+                host.instance_family = None
+            if instance_family is not None:
+                host.instance_family = instance_family
+                host.instance_type = None
+
+    def release_hosts(self, host_ids: List[str]) -> None:
+        for host_id in host_ids:
+            self.hosts[host_id].release()

+class HostsResponse(EC2BaseResponse):
+    def allocate_hosts(self):
+        params = self._get_params()
+        quantity = int(params.get("Quantity"))
+        host_recovery = params.get("HostRecovery")
+        zone = params.get("AvailabilityZone")
+        instance_type = params.get("InstanceType")
+        instance_family = params.get("InstanceFamily")
+        auto_placement = params.get("AutoPlacement")
+        tags = self._parse_tag_specification()
+        host_tags = tags.get("dedicated-host", {})
+        host_ids = self.ec2_backend.allocate_hosts(
+            quantity,
+            host_recovery,
+            zone,
+            instance_type,
+            instance_family,
+            auto_placement,
+            host_tags,
+        )
+        template = self.response_template(EC2_ALLOCATE_HOSTS)
+        return template.render(host_ids=host_ids)
+
+    def describe_hosts(self):
+        host_ids = list(self._get_params().get("HostId", {}).values())
+        filters = self._filters_from_querystring()
+        hosts = self.ec2_backend.describe_hosts(host_ids, filters)
+        template = self.response_template(EC2_DESCRIBE_HOSTS)
+        return template.render(account_id=self.current_account, hosts=hosts)
+
+    def modify_hosts(self):
+        params = self._get_params()
+        host_ids = list(self._get_params().get("HostId", {}).values())
+        auto_placement = params.get("AutoPlacement")
+        host_recovery = params.get("HostRecovery")
+        instance_type = params.get("InstanceType")
+        instance_family = params.get("InstanceFamily")
+        self.ec2_backend.modify_hosts(
+            host_ids, auto_placement, host_recovery, instance_type, instance_family
+        )
+        template = self.response_template(EC2_MODIFY_HOSTS)
+        return template.render(host_ids=host_ids)
+
+    def release_hosts(self):
+        host_ids = list(self._get_params().get("HostId", {}).values())
+        self.ec2_backend.release_hosts(host_ids)
+        template = self.response_template(EC2_RELEASE_HOSTS)
+        return template.render(host_ids=host_ids)

Each record includes the following fields: 'Date', 'Temperature', 'Humidity', 'Wind Speed', and 'Precipitation'. Your task is to write a Python function `aggregate_weather_data` that processes this file and returns a dictionary. The keys of this dictionary should be the names of the months (e.g., 'January'), and the values should be dictionaries containing the average values for 'Temperature', 'Humidity', 'Wind Speed', and 'Precipitation' for each month.

**Input Format:**
- The CSV file has a header row with the column names: 'Date', 'Temperature', 'Humidity', 'Wind Speed', 'Precipitation'.
- Each subsequent row contains the weather data for a specific day.
- The 'Date' column is in the format 'YYYY-MM-DD'.

**Output Format:**
- A dictionary where each key is a month name and each value is a dictionary with the average values for 'Temperature', 'Humidity', 'Wind Speed', and 'Precipitation'.

**Sample Input:**
```
Date,Temperature,Humidity,Wind Speed,Precipitation
2023-01-01,5.0,80.0,10.0,0.0
2023-01-02,6.0,85.0,12.0,0.0
2023-02-01,3.0,90.0,8.0,0.0
2023-02-02,4.0,95.0,10.0,0.0
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'January': {'Temperature': 5.5, 'Humidity': 82.5, 'Wind Speed': 11.0, 'Precipitation': 0.0},
    'February': {'Temperature': 3.5, 'Humidity': 92.5, 'Wind Speed': 9.0, 'Precipitation': 0.0}
}
```

```python
import csv
from datetime import datetime
from collections import defaultdict

def aggregate_weather_data(file_path):
    """
    Processes a CSV file containing weather data and returns a dictionary with monthly averages.

    Args:
        file_path (str): The path to the CSV file.

    Returns:
        dict: A dictionary with month names as keys and dictionaries of average weather data as values.
    """
    monthly_data = defaultdict(lambda: {'Temperature': [], 'Humidity': [], 'Wind Speed': [], 'Precipitation': []})
    
    with open(file_path, mode='r') as file:
        reader = csv.DictReader(file)
        for row in reader:
            date = datetime.strptime(row['Date'], '%Y-%m-%d')
            month_name = date.strftime('%B')
            
            monthly_data[month_name]['Temperature'].append(float(row['Temperature']))
            monthly_data[month_name]['Humidity'].append(float(row['Humidity']))
            monthly_data[month_name]['Wind Speed'].append(float(row['Wind Speed']))
            monthly_data[month_name]['Precipitation'].append(float(row['Precipitation']))
    
    monthly_averages = {}
    for month, data in monthly_data.items():
        monthly_averages[month] = {key: sum(values) / len(values) for key, values in data.items()}
    
    return monthly_averages

# Example usage:
# result = aggregate_weather_data('path_to_your_weather_data.csv')
# print(result)

<#sep#>You are tasked with writing a Python script to process a JSON file containing a list of books. Each book is represented as a dictionary with the following keys: `title`, `author`, `year`, and `publisher`. Your script must validate the JSON data according to the following rules:
- The `title` must be a non-empty string.
- The `author` must be a string.
- The `year` must be a positive integer not exceeding the current year.
- The `publisher` must be a string.

If a book entry is valid, it should be written to a new JSON file named `validated_books.json`. If a book entry is invalid, print an error message indicating the index of the book and the specific validation errors.

**Sample Input (books.json):**
```json
[
    {"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "year": 1949, "publisher": "Secker & Warburg"},
    {"title": "", "author": "J.K. Rowling", "year": 2005, "publisher": "Bloomsbury"},
    {"title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee", "year": 1960, "publisher": "J.B. Lippincott & Co."},
    {"title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "year": 1925, "publisher": "Charles Scribner's Sons"},
    {"title": "Pride and Prejudice", "author": "Jane Austen", "year": 1813, "publisher": "T. Egerton, Whitehall"},
    {"title": "The Catcher in the Rye", "author": "J.D. Salinger", "year": "1951", "publisher": "Little, Brown and Company"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Book at index 2 has errors: {'title': 'Title must be a non-empty string.'}
Book at index 6 has errors: {'year': 'Year must be a positive integer within a sensible range.'}
All other books are valid.
Validated books have been written to validated_books.json
```

```python
import json
from datetime import datetime

def is_valid_year(year):
    """Check if the year is a positive integer and within a sensible range."""
    return isinstance(year, int) and year > 0 and year <= datetime.now().year

def validate_book(book):
    """Validate a single book entry and return a dictionary of errors."""
    errors = {}
    
    if not isinstance(book.get('title', ''), str) or not book['title'].strip():
        errors['title'] = "Title must be a non-empty string."
    
    if not isinstance(book.get('author', ''), str):
        errors['author'] = "Author must be a string."
        
    if not is_valid_year(book.get('year')):
        errors['year'] = "Year must be a positive integer within a sensible range."
        
    if not isinstance(book.get('publisher', ''), str):
        errors['publisher'] = "Publisher must be a string."
    
    return errors

def read_books_data(filename):
    """Reads book data from a JSON file and return a list of books."""
    try:
        with open(filename, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            data = json.load(file)
        return data
    except (FileNotFoundError, json.JSONDecodeError) as e:
        print(f"Error reading JSON file: {e}")
        return []

def write_valid_books_data(books, filename):
    """Writes validated book data to a new JSON file."""
    with open(filename, 'w', encoding='utf-8') as file:
        json.dump(books, file, ensure_ascii=False, indent=4)

def main():
    """Main function to process the books data."""
    input_filename = 'books.json'
    output_filename = 'validated_books.json'
    
    books = read_books_data(input_filename)
    validated_books = []
    invalid_detail = []

    for idx, book in enumerate(books, start=1):
        errors = validate_book(book)
        if not errors:
            validated_books.append(book)
        else:
            invalid_detail.append({
                "book_index": idx,
                "errors": errors
            })

    if invalid_detail:
        for detail in invalid_detail:
            print(f"Book at index {detail['book_index']} has errors: {detail['errors']}")
    else:
        print("All books are valid.")

    # Write only validated books to the new JSON file
    write_valid_books_data(validated_books, output_filename)
    print(f"Validated books have been written to {output_filename}")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()

There is a special n × n matrix A, you should calculate its permanent modulo 1000000007 (109 + 7). The special property of matrix A is almost all its elements equal to 1. Only k elements have specified value.

You can find the definition of permanent at the link: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent
Input

The first line contains two space-separated integers n, k (1 ≤ n ≤ 105; 1 ≤ k ≤ 50).

The next k lines contain the description of the matrix. The i-th line contains three space-separated integers xi, yi, wi (1 ≤ xi, yi ≤ n; 0 ≤ wi ≤ 109). These numbers denote that Axi, yi = wi. All the elements of the matrix except of the given elements are equal to 1.

It's guaranteed that all the positions (xi, yi) are distinct.

Output

Print the permanent of the matrix modulo 1000000007 (109 + 7).

Examples

Input

3 1
1 1 2


Output

8

gpt: <think> Okay, so I have this problem where I need to calculate the permanent of a special n x n matrix modulo 1e9+7. The matrix is mostly filled with 1s, except for k elements which have specific values. The challenge here is that calculating the permanent directly is #P-complete, which is really hard, but since k is up to 50, maybe there's a way to handle this with some clever combinatorics or dynamic programming.

First, let me recall what the permanent of a matrix is. Oh right, the permanent of an n x n matrix A is the sum over all permutations of the products of elements: perm(A) = sum_{σ ∈ S_n} product_{i=1 to n} A_{i, σ(i)}. So each permutation contributes one term, which multiplies the elements along the permutation's mapping. The problem is that for n up to 1e5, it's impossible to compute this by enumerating all permutations directly, as that would be way too slow even for n=100.

But the key here is that almost all elements are 1. Only k elements are different from 1. Maybe those non-1 elements can be considered as the "special" parts that contribute more significantly, and the rest 1s can be factored into the product somehow.

Wait, if all the elements except the k were 1, then the permanent would just be n! because each permutation would contribute 1 multiplied n times (since all terms are 1), summed over n! permutations. But here, exactly the k elements are not 1, so each permutation's contribution could be the product of those elements along its path, multiplied by the rest (the 1s). Hmm, but I need to see how to efficiently compute this considering the non-1 elements.

Alternatively, perhaps we can model the problem using inclusion-exclusion or some other combinatorial technique specific to sparse matrices where only a few elements are different from the base element (here, 1).

Let me think again: since all the elements not in the given positions are 1, the permanent can be written as the sum over all permutations of the product: product_{i not in any (xi, yi)} 1 multiplied by the product of the w_i for the permutation elements that hit the given positions. Wait, actually, more accurately: for each permutation σ, the term is the product over i=1..n of ( A_{i,σ(i)} ). Since all entries except those in the given k positions are 1, so the product is equal to the product of the w's for the positions (i, σ(i)) that are among the k elements. For the others, it's 1. So the permanent is the sum over all permutations σ of the product of the w_i of those cells in the permutation's path that were specified.

Now, the challenge is to compute this sum efficiently given that k is small (50).

Hmm, this is similar to the problem of inclusion exclusion for overlapping rectangles but here in terms of permutations.

Wait, but even with that, how can we model this?

Maybe we can think of it as the permanent of the matrix which has 1's in almost all positions except for some specified ones. Since the permanent is the sum over permutations, the terms where the permutations avoid all the special positions contribute exactly 1 each, and then the terms where the permutations pick some of the non-1 elements contribute additional factors. But even this line of thinking might not directly help.

Alternatively, perhaps it's better to model this using generating functions. Let's think of the permanent as a sum over all possible ways where the permutation can choose to include a subset of the non-1 elements. Wait, but permutations are selections with constraints (each element exactly once in the permutation).

Alternatively, since the non-1 elements are a relatively small number, perhaps the overall permutations that actually hit any of these positions can be considered as contributing terms with certain multiplicative factors, and the rest contribute as if there were no exceptions (i.e., 1s all over). But the problem is that permutations can't have cycles or other structures that are hard to account for. Hmm.

Alternatively, perhaps the approach is to model this as the total permanent of the matrix with all entries equal to 1, plus some correction terms from the k non-1 elements.

Wait, let's see:

The standard formula for a matrix with all 1's except for some entries:

The permanent with those positions set to 1 would be n!.

Wait, no: If the entire matrix is all 1s, then permanent is indeed n!.

Now, here, in our case, the given elements have wi terms instead of 1, so we need to compute the permanent of (matrix where except the k entries, all others are 1). Let's denote the matrix M such that M[i][j] = 1 except for the given k terms which are wi's.

Let me see: if those k elements are the only non-ones, then any permutation's contribution includes all the 1s except when the permutation selects one of those non-1 elements.

Thus the permanent can be written as the sum over permutations σ of product_{i=1 to n} M[i][σ(i)].

Each term in the sum is equal to the product over all positions (i,σ(i)) where, for each (xi,yi), if (i,y) corresponds to their position, then multiply by wi instead of 1.

Hmm. So the problem reduces to considering all permutations, and for each permutation, multiply by the product of the wi's for which their (i,j) is included in the specified (xi,yi) pairs such that in the permutation, at position i, the permutation maps to that j (i.e., σ(i) = j). So this is the product over the terms where (i, j) is in the specified list (xi,yi) and j = σ(i), multiplied by all the 1s for everything else.

But how do we compute this efficiently?

Thinking small cases first, consider when k is 0. Then the permanent is 1^n per permutation, so n!.

If k is 1: Let's say the matrix has exactly one entry w1 at (x,y). Then the permanent is (n-1)! * (w1 + (n^2 -1)*0+...? Wait no. Wait, more precisely, the total permanent can be considered as the sum over all permutations σ either selects the element (x, y) in their permutation or not.

The number of permutations that include (x, y) as an element (i.e., σ(x) = y) is (n-1)! : because after fixing σ(x) to y, the remaining n-1 elements form a permutation of the remaining positions.

For each such permutation, the term includes that wi where (x, y) is selected. Additionally, the terms not involving (x,y) contribute normally, since their elements are all 1s except the one w1?

Wait, no: all other entries not specified are 1, so if the permutation doesn't select the single non-1 element (x,y), then the entire product is still 1*1*...*1 multiplied by 1 except the (x,y) term which is not selected here, so the term is 1. I've found a workaround in the form of explicitly calling the `setUp`/`tearDown` methods from the subclasses, but I don't think this it's obvious/intuitive to users that they'd need to do this. I suspect most would just give up and declare it broken/bugged. It was only because I knew it used to work in earlier versions and went hunting for a changelog that I read about the change in behaviour for the class level decorator and thought to give this a go.
<details><summary>click to expand code with workaround applied</summary>
<p>

```python
import unittest

import boto3
from moto import mock_s3


@mock_s3
class BaseTest(unittest.TestCase):

    def setUp(self) -> None:
        self.s3 = boto3.resource('s3')
        self.test_bucket = self.s3.Bucket("testbucket")
        self.test_bucket.create()

    def tearDown(self) -> None:
        self.test_bucket.delete()


@mock_s3
class MyTest(BaseTest):

    def setUp(self) -> None:
        super().setUp()

    def tearDown(self) -> None:
        super().tearDown()

    def test_a_thing(self):
        pass

```
</p>
</details>

<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple library management system. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Add a Book**: Add a book to the library with its title and author.
2. **Borrow a Book**: Borrow a book from the library if it is available.
3. **Return a Book**: Return a book to the library if it was borrowed.
4. **Display Books**: Display all books in the library along with their availability status.

Your program should handle the following commands:
- `ADD <title> <author>`: Adds a book with the given title and author to the library.
- `BORROW <title> <author>`: Borrows a book with the given title and author if it is available.
- `RETURN <title> <author>`: Returns a book with the given title and author if it was borrowed.
- `DISPLAY`: Displays all books in the library.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `N` (1 ≤ N ≤ 100), the number of commands.
- The next `N` lines contain commands in the format described above.

**Output:**
- For each command, print the appropriate message as described in the operations.

**Sample Input:**
```
6
ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell
ADD BraveNewWorld AldousHuxley
BORROW 1984 GeorgeOrwell
DISPLAY
RETURN 1984 GeorgeOrwell
DISPLAY
```

**Sample Output:**
```
The book '1984' by GeorgeOrwell has been added to the library.
The book 'BraveNewWorld' by AldousHuxley has been added to the library.
The book '1984' by GeorgeOrwell has been borrowed.
'1984' by GeorgeOrwell (Borrowed: Yes)
'BraveNewWorld' by AldousHuxley (Borrowed: No)
The book '1984' by GeorgeOrwell has been returned.
'1984' by GeorgeOrwell (Borrowed: No)
'BraveNewWorld' by AldousHuxley (Borrowed: No)
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author):
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.is_borrowed = False

    def borrow(self):
        """Borrow the book if it is not already borrowed."""
        if not self.is_borrowed:
            self.is_borrowed = True
            return f"The book '{self.title}' by {self.author} has been borrowed."
        else:
            return f"The book '{self.title}' by {self.author} is already borrowed."

    def return_book(self):
        """Return the book if it was borrowed."""
        if self.is_borrowed:
            self.is_borrowed = False
            return f"The book '{self.title}' by {self.author} has been returned."
        else:
            return f"The book '{self.title}' by {self.author} was not borrowed."

    def __str__(self):
        """Return a string representation of the book with its availability status."""
        return f"'{self.title}' by {self.author} (Borrowed: {'Yes' if self.is_borrowed else 'No'})"


class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = []

    def add_book(self, title, author):
        """Add a new book to the library."""
        new_book = Book(title, author)
        self.books.append(new_book)
        return f"The book '{title}' by {author} has been added to the library."

    def borrow_book(self, title, author):
        """Borrow a book from the library if it is available."""
        for book in self.books:
            if book.title == title and book.author == author:
                return book.borrow()
        return f"The book '{title}' by {author} is not in the library."

    def return_book(self, title, author):
        """Return a book to the library if it was borrowed."""
        for book in self.books:
            if book.title == title and book.author == author:
                return book.return_book()
        return f"The book '{title}' by {author} is not in the library."

    def display_books(self):
        """Display all books in the library with their availability status."""
        if not self.books:
            return "The library is currently empty."
        else:
            return "\n".join(str(book) for book in self.books)


# Example usage:
library = Library()
commands = [
    "ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell",
    "ADD BraveNewWorld AldousHuxley",
    "BORROW 1984 GeorgeOrwell",
    "DISPLAY",
    "RETURN 1984 GeorgeOrwell",
    "DISPLAY"
]

for command in commands:
    parts = command.split()
    if parts[0] == "ADD":
        print(library.add_book(parts[1], parts[2]))
    elif parts[0] == "BORROW":
        print(library.borrow_book(parts[1], parts[2]))
    elif parts[0] == "RETURN":
        print(library.return_book(parts[1], parts[2]))
    elif parts[0] == "DISPLAY":
        print(library.display_books())

So Q(1) can take various integer values depending on the coefficients of P(x). But given that P(x) has no real roots, what constraints does that impose on the coefficients?

First, P(x) must be always positive or always negative. Since it's quartic with leading term c4 x^4, which dominates for large |x|. So if c4 >0, then P(x) >0 for all x, and if c4 <0, then P(x) <0 for all x. Therefore, c4 cannot be zero (since degree is 4). So c4 is a non-zero integer.

Moreover, for the polynomial to have no real roots, it must be positive definite (if c4 >0) or negative definite (if c4 <0). Therefore, the coefficients must satisfy certain inequalities. For example, P(x) must be always positive or always negative.

Total perm = (number of permutations avoiding (x,y)) * 1 + (number of permutations using (x,y)) * w(x,y).

The number not picking it is n! - (number picking it).

original total when everything was 1 is n!.

To replace (x,y) from 1 to w will add an extra of (number of permutations hitting that position) times (w-1).

Therefore the result is n! + (number hitting that position)(w-1).

Because, for those permutations which hit the position, their product becomes (original 1) multiplied by (w) instead of 1, so each such permutation contributes (w-1) more than before.

Therefore:

perm = n! + (n-1)! * (w - 1).

Ah, right, so this is correct. For k=1.

So for multiple elements, perhaps there is a way to compute this incrementally?

Wait, but the problem with multiple elements is that the permutations can pick more than one of the elements. That complicates things because the terms where permutations pass through several special elements can have their product as the product of those wi's. This makes a direct inclusion approach non-trivial because there are overlaps.

In the inclusion-exclusion problem, each time a permutation crosses one of the special cells, the contribution is multiplied by their wi. But the permutations that cross multiple special cells contribute the product of all the wi.

Hence, the problem becomes similar to a 'weighted' problem where each of the k elements can be either chosen on the path (contributing their wi instead of 1) or not (so 1). The problem is the sum over all possible permutations of the product of the chosen elements. Since each element is a cell (x,y), choosing to include it in the permutation's path (i.e., σ(x)=y) contributes wi, except that the permutation can't choose more than one cell from the same row or column (since permutations are bijections).

This complicates things, but since k is small (up to 50), perhaps there's a way to model this with dynamic programming.

Alternatively, perhaps we can model this as the permanent of a modified matrix where the k elements are 'activated', and find a way to express the permanent in terms of the k elements?

Hmm. The idea is that the permanent of a matrix where almost all entries are 1 except some is the same as the permanent of the all-1 matrix plus some correction terms from the non-one entries. But it's not linear, so I can't just add them directly.

Alternatively, perhaps express the permanent as the number of fixed permutations times 1 (the all-1 case) plus contributions from all subsets of the special cells where the permutation selects exactly those cells, multiplied by the product of the wi's for those cells.

Wait, the permanent is exactly the sum over all subsets of the special cells that form a partial permutation (i.e., no two cells share a row or column, and they form a subset of a permutation's choices). For each such subset S (a partial matching), the generating function is the product of their w_i, and the remaining part of the permutation (the remaining rows and columns) is a permutation on n - |S| points. So the total contribution for subset S is ( (n-|S|)! ) * (product of w_i over S elements). But this is only possible if the subset S can be extended to a permutation, which requires that S has no overlapping rows or columns and can be part of a permutation.

Wait, perhaps the idea is to model the contribution to the permanent by selecting a subset of the given elements such that they don't conflict (i.e., no two in the same row or column), then for each such subset, multiply the product of their w's by the permanent of the remaining (1-only) submatrix.

Wait, but the product of the subset's w's and the rest's 1s need to be considered.

Wait, more precisely: each permutation can be viewed as choosing some non-overlapping set of cells from the k (none, one, or some, as long as they don't share rows or columns), and then permuting the remaining.

Thus, the permanent can be expressed as the sum over all possible subsets of the k special cells, where the subset is a partial permutation (i.e., each row and column is used at most once), and for each such subset S, the contribution is (product of w's in S) multiplied by (n - |S|)!.

Then the total is the sum over all possible subsets S (satisfying the partial permutation condition) of (product_{(i,j) in S} w_{i,j}) times ( (n - |S|)! ).

Ah! This is an important insight. The key is that any permutation can be considered as a superset of such a partial selection S. So the permanent is exactly the sum over all possible subsets S (S is a partial permutation) of their contributions. Because for each such subset S, the entries in S are part of the permutation's path, and the remaining must form a permutation on the rest.

Therefore, the problem reduces to enumerating all possible subsets S of the given cells such that no two share a row or column (they form a partial permutation matrix), then computing their contributions and adding them together.

The problem now is, since k is up to 50 but the subset S can be any subset of the k cells forming a partial permutation, the number of such subsets might be manageable?

Wait for k=50, the possible subsets of the cells with no overlaps would be similar to selecting an independent set on the rows and columns. But perhaps using the fact that each subset must be an independent set in terms of not overlapping rows or columns, the total number of possible such subsets can be bounded exponentially, but with k=50, perhaps we can process this via inclusion.

Alternatively, think recursively: to compute the total sum, which is the sum over subsets S (over all possible compatible subsets of the given cells) of (product of their weights * (n - s)! ), where s = |S|. So this can be<#sep#>DynamoDB - Provide full cancellation reasons for write_transactions failue
When using boto3 and `write_transactions`, if the transaction fails we normally get an error response that includes the full cancellation reasons: 

```
"CancellationReasons": [
            {
                "Code": "None"
            },
            {
                "Code": "ConditionalCheckFailed",
                "Message": "The conditional request failed"
            },
            {
                "Code": "None"
            }
        ]
```

when using moto (version 2.3.0) this object is not returned on transaction failure, we just get the error message: 
`"Error with transaction: An error occurred (TransactionCanceledException) when calling the TransactWriteItems operation: Transaction cancelled, please refer cancellation reasons for specific reasons [None, ConditionalCheckFailed, None]"`

Would be great to get the full cancellation reasons so we can better test the our transaction error handling. 

Thanks!

         indexes_by_name = dict((i.name, i) for i in all_indexes<#sep#>You are given a list of cities represented as points in a 2D plane. Your task is to find an approximate solution to the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) using a greedy algorithm. The algorithm should start from the first city and repeatedly visit the nearest unvisited city until all cities are visited, then return to the starting city. Implement the function `optimize_route` that takes a list of cities and a distance function as input and returns the order of cities to visit and the total distance of the route.

**Input:**
- A list of cities, where each city is represented as a tuple of two integers (x, y).
- A distance function that takes two cities and returns the distance between them.

**Output:**
- A tuple containing the order of cities to visit and the total distance of the route.

**Sample Input:**
```python
cities = [(0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 5), (3, 6), (6, 8)]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Path: [(0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 5), (3, 6), (6, 8), (0, 0)]
Total Distance: 22.47213595499958
```

```python
import numpy as np

def optimize_route(cities, distance_func):
    """
    Approximate the TSP using a Nearest Neighbor greedy algorithm.
    
    :param cities: List of cities (nodes) represented as tuples of (x, y) coordinates.
    :param distance_func: Function that takes two cities and returns the distance between them.
    :return: Tuple (path, total_distance) where path is the order of cities to visit and total_distance is the total distance traveled.
    """
    start_city = cities[0]
    path = [start_city]
    unvisited_cities = set(cities[1:])
    total_distance = 0.0
    
    current_city = start_city
    while unvisited_cities:
        nearest_city = min(unvisited_cities, key=lambda city: distance_func(current_city, city))
        total_distance += distance_func(current_city, nearest_city)
        path.append(nearest_city)
        unvisited_cities.remove(nearest_city)
        current_city = nearest_city
        
    total_distance += distance_func(path[-1], start_city)
    path.append(start_city)
    
    return path, total_distance

def euclidean_distance(city1, city2):
    """
    Compute the Euclidean distance between two cities.
    
    :param city1: Tuple (x, y) coordinates of the first city.
    :param city2: Tuple (x, y) coordinates of the second city.
    :return: Euclidean distance between city1 and city2.
    """
    return np.sqrt((city1[0] - city2[0])**2 + (city1[1] - city2[1])**2)

# Example usage
cities = [(0, 0), (1, 1), (2, 5), (3, 6), (6, 8)]
path, distance = optimize_route(cities, euclidean_distance)
print("Path:", path)
print("Total Distance:", distance)

+            raise Table<#sep#>You are developing a REST API for a blogging platform. The API must support the following operations:

1. **List all blog posts**: Return a JSON array of all blog posts.
2. **Retrieve a single blog post by ID**: Return a JSON object of the blog post with the specified ID. If the post does not exist, return a 404 error.
3. **Create a new blog post**: Accept a JSON object with `title` and `content` fields and add it to the blog. Return the created blog post with a 201 status code. If the request is invalid, return a 400 error.
4. **Update an existing blog post**: Accept a JSON object with optional `title` and `content` fields and update the blog post with the specified ID. Return the updated blog post. If the post does not exist, return a 404 error. If the request is invalid, return a 400 error.
5. **Delete a blog post**: Remove the blog post with the specified ID. Return a 204 status code. If the post does not exist, return a 404 error.

The API should use appropriate HTTP methods and return JSON formatted data.

**Sample Input/Output:**

- **GET /posts**
  - Response: `[{ "id": 1, "title": "First Post", "content": "This is the content of the first post" }, { "id": 2, "title": "Second Post", "content": "This is the content of the second post" }]`

- **GET /posts/1**
  - Response: `{ "id": 1, "title": "First Post", "content": "This is the content of the first post" }`

- **POST /posts**
  - Request: `{ "title": "Third Post", "content": "This is the content of the third post" }`
  - Response: `{ "id": 3, "title": "Third Post", "content": "This is the content of the third post" }`

- **PUT /posts/1**
  - Request: `{ "title": "Updated First Post" }`
  - Response: `{ "id": 1, "title": "Updated First Post", "content": "This is the content of the first post" }`

- **DELETE /posts/2**
  - Response: `204 No Content`

```python
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify, abort
from flask_restful import Api, Resource

app = Flask(__name__)
api = Api(app)

# Simulated in-memory database of blog posts
posts = [
    {'id': 1, 'title': 'First Post', 'content': 'This is the content of the first post'},
    {'id': 2, 'title': 'Second Post', 'content': 'This is the content of the second post'},
]

def find_post(post_id):
    """Helper function to find a post by its ID."""
    return next((post for post in posts if post['id'] == post_id), None)

class Post(Resource):
    def get(self, post_id=None):
        """Retrieve all posts or a single post by ID."""
        if post_id is None:
            return jsonify(posts)
        post = find_post(post_id)
        if post is None:
            abort(404, description="Post not found")
        return jsonify(post)

    def post(self):
        """Create a new post."""
        if not request.json or 'title' not in request.json or 'content' not in request.json:
            abort(400, description="Invalid request: title and content are required fields")
        new_post = {
            'id': posts[-1]['id'] + 1 if len(posts) > 0 else 1,
            'title': request.json['title'],
            'content': request.json['content']
        }
        posts.append(new_post)
        return jsonify(new_post), 201

    def put(self, post_id):
        """Update an existing post."""
        post = find_post(post_id)
        if post is None:
            abort(404, description="Post not found")
        if not request.json:
            abort(400, description="Invalid request")
        post['title'] = request.json.get('title', post['title'])
        post['content'] = request.json.get('content', post['content'])
        return jsonify(post)

    def delete(self, post_id):
        """Delete a post."""
        global posts
        post = find_post(post_id)
        if post is None:
            abort(404, description="Post not found")
        posts = [p for p in posts if p['id'] != post_id]
        return '', 204

api.add_resource(Post, '/posts', '/posts/<int:post_id>')

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)

"This is a sample text that needs to be summarized. Simplifying the information to convey the main points is what summarization is about. The function should be versatile enough to handle different text lengths and styles."
```

```python
import nltk
from nltk.corpus import stopwords
from nltk.tokenize import word_tokenize, sent_tokenize
from collections import defaultdict
import heapq

# Ensure the NLTK resources are downloaded
nltk.download('punkt')
nltk.download('stopwords')

def generate_summary(text: str, max_length: int) -> str:
    """
    Generates a summary of the given text with a length not exceeding max_length characters.
    Uses the Natural Language Toolkit (NLTK) library for text processing.
    
    :param text: The input text to be summarized.
    :param max_length: The maximum length of the summary.
    :return: A summary of the text with length not exceeding max_length characters.
    """
    # Tokenize the text into sentences
    sentences = sent_tokenize(text)
    
    # Tokenize the text into words and remove stopwords
    stop_words = set(stopwords.words("english"))
    words = word_tokenize(text.lower())
    filtered_words = [word for word in words if word.isalnum() and word not in stop_words]
    
    # Calculate word frequency
    word_frequencies = defaultdict(int)
    for word in filtered_words:
        word_frequencies[word] += 1
    
    # Calculate sentence scores based on word frequencies
    sentence_scores = defaultdict(int)
    for sentence in sentences:
        for word in word_tokenize(sentence.lower()):
            if word in word_frequencies:
                sentence_scores[sentence] += word_frequencies[word]
    
    # Extract top sentences using heap queue to get the highest scores
    summarized_sentences = heapq.nlargest(5, sentence_scores, key=sentence_scores.get)
    
    # Join the summarized sentences
    summary = ' '.join(summarized_sentences)
    
    # If summary is longer than max_length, truncate it
    if len(summary) > max_length:
        summary = summary[:max_length]
    else:
        # If the summary can be longer, add more sentences until we reach max_length
        for sentence in sentences:
            if sentence not in summarized_sentences:
                potential_summary = summary + ' ' + sentence
                if len(potential_summary) > max_length:
                    break
                summary = potential_summary
    
    return summary.strip()

-        try:
-            item = self.dynamodb<#sep#>Implement a stack data structure in Python using a class. Your stack should support the following operations:
- `is_empty()`: Returns `True` if the stack is empty, otherwise `False`.
- `push(item)`: Adds an item to the top of the stack.
- `pop()`: Removes and returns the item from the top of the stack. If the stack is empty, raise an `IndexError` with the message "pop from empty stack".
- `peek()`: Returns the top item of the stack without removing it. If the stack is empty, raise an `IndexError` with the message "peek from empty stack".
- `size()`: Returns the number of items in the stack.
- `__str__()`: Returns a string representation of the stack.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Input:
stack = Stack()
stack.push(1)
stack.push(2)
stack.push(3)
print("Stack:", stack)  # Stack: [1, 2, 3]
print("Top item:", stack.peek())  # Top item: 3
print("Stack size:", stack.size())  # Stack size: 3
print("First pop:", stack.pop())  # First pop: 3
print("Stack after pop:", stack)  # Stack after pop: [1, 2]

Output:
Stack: [1, 2, 3]
Top item: 3
Stack size: 3
First pop: 3
Stack after pop: [1, 2]
```

```python
class Stack:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty list to hold the stack items."""
        self.items = []

    def is_empty(self):
        """Check if the stack is empty."""
        return len(self.items) == 0

    def push(self, item):
        """Add an item to the top of the stack."""
        self.items.append(item)

    def pop(self):
        """Remove and return the item from the top of the stack.
        Raise an error if the stack is empty."""
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("pop from empty stack")
        return self.items.pop()

    def peek(self):
        """Return the top item of the stack without removing it.
        Raise an error if the stack is empty."""
        if self.is_empty():
            raise IndexError("peek from empty stack")
        return self.items[-1]

    def size(self):
        """Return the number of items in the stack."""
        return len(self.items)

    def __str__(self):
        """Return a string representation of the stack."""
        return str(self.items)


# Example Usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    stack = Stack()
    stack.push(1)
    stack.push(2)
    stack.push(3)
    print("Stack:", stack)  # Stack: [1, 2, 3]
    print("Top item:", stack.peek())  # Top item: 3
    print("Stack size:", stack.size())  # Stack size: 3
    print("First pop:", stack.pop())  # First pop: 3
    print("Stack after pop:", stack)  # Stack after pop: [1, 2]

                     else:
                         range_condition = key_conditions.get<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `CircularQueue` class in Python, which should support the following operations:
- `enqueue(element)`: Adds an element to the end of the queue. Raises an exception if the queue is full.
- `dequeue()`: Removes and returns the element at the front of the queue. Raises an exception if the queue is empty.
- `peek()`: Returns the element at the front of the queue without removing it. Raises an exception if the queue is empty.
- `is_empty()`: Returns `True` if the queue is empty, otherwise `False`.
- `size()`: Returns the number of elements in the queue.

The queue should handle edge cases such as when the queue is full or empty. The queue should be initialized with a fixed capacity.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
cq = CircularQueue(3)
cq.enqueue(1)
cq.enqueue(2)
cq.enqueue(3)
print(cq.dequeue())  # Output: 1
print(cq.peek())     # Output: 2
print(cq.size())     # Output: 2
```

```python
class CircularQueue:
    def __init__(self, capacity):
        """
        Initializes the CircularQueue with a fixed capacity.
        """
        self.capacity = capacity
        self.queue = [None] * capacity
        self.head = 0
        self.tail = 0
        self.count = 0

    def enqueue(self, element):
        """
        Adds an element to the end of the queue.
        Raises an exception if the queue is full.
        """
        if self.is_full():
            raise Exception("Queue is full")
        self.queue[self.tail] = element
        self.tail = (self.tail + 1) % self.capacity
        self.count += 1

    def dequeue(self):
        """
        Removes and returns the element at the front of the queue.
        Raises an exception if the queue is empty.
        """
        if self.is_empty():
            raise Exception("Queue is empty")
        element = self.queue[self.head]
        self.queue[self.head] = None
        self.head = (self.head + 1) % self.capacity
        self.count -= 1
        return element

    def peek(self):
        """
        Returns the element at the front of the queue without removing it.
        Raises an exception if the queue is empty.
        """
        if self.is_empty():
            raise Exception("Queue is empty")
        return self.queue[self.head]

    def is_empty(self):
        """
        Returns True if the queue is empty, otherwise False.
        """
        return self.count == 0

    def is_full(self):
        """
        Returns True if the queue is full, otherwise False.
        """
        return self.count == self.capacity

    def size(self):
        """
        Returns the number of elements in the queue.
        """
        return self.count
```<#sep#>^2 + 6c_3 x + 2c_2 \quad \Rightarrow \quad P''(1) = 12c_4 + 6c_3 + 2c_2
\]
\[
P'''(x) = 24c_4 x + 6c_3 \quad \Rightarrow \quad P'''(1) = 24c_4 + 6c_3
\]
\[
P''''(x) = 24c_4 \quad \Rightarrow \quad P''''(1) = 24c_4
\]

Substituting these into the expression for \( Q(x) \) and evaluating at \( x = 1 \):
\[
Q(1) = P(1) + 2P'(1) + 4P''(1) + 8P'''(1) + 16P''''(1)
\]
\[
Q(1) = (c_4 + c_3 + c_2 + c_1 + c_0) + 2(4c_4 + 3c_3 + 2c_2 + c_1) + 4(12c_4 + 6c_3 + 2c_2) + 8(24c_4 + 6c_3) + 16(24c_4)
\]

Simplifying each term:
\[
Q(1) = c_4 + c_3 + c_2 + c_1 + c_0 + 8c_4 + 6c_3 + 4c_2 + 2c_1 + 48c_4 + 24c_3 + 8c_2 + 192c_4 + 48c_3 + 384c_4
\]

Combining like terms:
\[
Q(1) = (1 + 8 + 48 + 192 + 384)c_4 + (1 + 6 + 24 + 48)c_3 + (1 + 4 + 8)c_2 + (1 + 2)c_1 + c_0
\]
\[
Q(1) = 633c_4 + 79c_3 + 13c_2 + 3c_1 + c_0
\]

Given that \( P(x) \) has no real roots, it must be either always positive or always negative. This implies that for any polynomial \( P(x) \) with coefficients \( c_4, c_3, c_2, c_1, c_0 \), the polynomial \( -P(x) \) (which has coefficients \( -c_4, -c_3, -c_2, -c_1, -c_0 \)) is also valid and will have \( Q(1) \) values that are the negatives of the original polynomial's \( Q(1) \).

Thus, every possible value of \( Q(1) \) has a corresponding negative value. The set of all possible values of \( Q(1) \) is symmetric around zero, leading to their sum being zero.

\[
\boxed{0}
\]<#sep#>₂,y₂) with weight w₂. 

The total permanent is:

per(M) = (n! ) + (n-1)!*(w₁-1) + (n-1)!*(w₂-1) + (product of (w₁ -1)(w₂ -1)) * number of permutations that include both edges if they are not conflicting (i.e., their rows are different and columns are different).

Thus generally, the total is the sum over all subsets of the modified cells that form a matching, multiplied by the product of (w_i -1) and then multiplied by (n - m)! ?

The new matrix is the same except for certain cells set to w₁, w₂, etc.

Therefore, the permanent of the new matrix is:

Original permanent (n!) +

Similarly for triplets of non-conflicting edges, etc.

Where S(σ) is the subset of special edges used in the permutation.

def dp(e_index, used_rows, used_columns):

   if all edges have been processed (e_index == k):

       return the current_m's sum (product of selected edges weights)

   else:

       current edge is E[e_index], let's say has row_r and column_c.

       # option1: not take this edge: compute total from rest.

       option1 = dp(e_index +1, used_rows, used_columns).

       # option2: take it, but only if not already used:

       if (row_r not in used_rows) and (column_c not in used_columns):

           new_used_rows = set(used_rows) | {row_r}

           new_used_columns = set(used_columns) | {column_c}

           option2 = E[e_index].weight * dp(e_index+1, new_used_rows, new_used_columns)

       else:

           option2 =0

       return option1 + option2

But this is for enumerating all possible matchings, accumulating the products. Although, this would be exponential because for k=50 it would require many steps. Even if the base cases are small, it can take up to 2^50 steps.

The program should select a random word from a predefined list and prompt the user to guess letters of the word. The user has a maximum of 6 incorrect guesses. The game should display the current state of the word after each guess, with guessed letters revealed and unguessed letters hidden. If the user correctly guesses all the letters of the word, output a congratulatory message. If the user exhausts all their guesses without guessing the word, output the correct word and a message indicating the user has lost.

**Input:**
- The user will input a single letter at a time during the game.

**Output:**
- The program will display the current state of the word after each guess.
- After the game ends, the program will print either a congratulatory message or the correct word and a loss message.

**Sample Interaction:**
```
Welcome to Hangman!
Current word: _ _ _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: P
Good guess!
Current word: P _ _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: Y
Good guess!
Current word: P Y _ _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: T
Good guess!
Current word: P Y T _ _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: H
Good guess!
Current word: P Y T H _ _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: O
Good guess!
Current word: P Y T H O _
Attempts remaining: 6
Guess a letter: N
Congratulations! You've guessed the word: P Y T H O N
```

```python
import random

def get_random_word(words):
    """
    Selects and returns a random word from the provided list, converted to uppercase.
    
    :param words: List of words to choose from.
    :return: A randomly selected word in uppercase.
    """
    return random.choice(words).upper()

def play_hangman():
    """
    Plays a game of Hangman where the user guesses letters of a randomly selected word.
    The user has a maximum of 6 incorrect guesses.
    """
    word_list = ['python', 'developer', 'hangman', 'challenge', 'project', 'algorithm']
    word_to_guess = get_random_word(word_list)
    guessed_letters = set()
    attempts_remaining = 6
    word_display = ['_'] * len(word_to_guess)

    print("Welcome to Hangman!")
    while attempts_remaining > 0 and '_' in word_display:
        print(f"Current word: {' '.join(word_display)}")
        print(f"Attempts remaining: {attempts_remaining}")
        guess = input("Guess a letter: ").upper()
        
        if len(guess) != 1 or not guess.isalpha():
            print("Please enter a single alphabetic character.")
            continue
        
        if guess in guessed_letters:
            print("You've already guessed that letter.")
            continue
        
        guessed_letters.add(guess)
        
        if guess in word_to_guess:
            for idx, letter in enumerate(word_to_guess):
                if letter == guess:
                    word_display[idx] = guess
            print("Good guess!")
        else:
            attempts_remaining -= 1
            print("Incorrect guess.")
    
    if '_' not in word_display:
        print(f"Congratulations! You've guessed the word: {' '.join(word_display)}")
    else:
        print(f"Sorry, you've run out of attempts. The word was: {word_to_guess}")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    play_hangman()
